Proof of the Einstein quadrupole formula for solutions of the Einstein-Vlasov system close to Minkowski spacetime
Érik Amorim

Abstract
We rigorously derive the quadrupole formula within the context of the Einstein-Vlasov system. The main contribution of this work is an estimate of the remainder terms, derived from well-defined assumptions, with explicitly stated error terms that depend on the solution’s boundedness and decay properties, and the distance to the source. The assumptions are linked to established properties of global solutions of the Einstein-Vlasov system as in [ 9 ] . Prior derivations of the quadrupole formula have relied on post-Newtonian analysis and lacked comparisons with global solution properties. The importance of the no-incoming-radiation condition is emphasized underscoring the need for solutions satisfying this condition. This work thus addresses the limitations of existing results and provides motivation for further research on global solution properties of the Einstein-Vlasov system.



†
†

Mathematics Subject Classification: 83C35, 83C05

1
Introduction and main result

Einstein’s theory of general relativity, formulated more than a century ago, revolutionized
our understanding of gravity and predicted the existence of
gravitational waves-a groundbreaking achievement that remained unconfirmed until
the historic detection by LIGO in 2015. Among the various aspects of general relativity,
the quadrupole formula stands as a fundamental cornerstone, providing a theoretical framework
to understand the emission of gravitational waves from massive, accelerating objects.

First introduced by Einstein in 1918 as a prediction of his general theory of relativity,
the quadrupole formula provides a crucial link between the dynamics of massive,
accelerating objects and the emission of gravitational radiation. This formula gained
renewed attention with the discovery of the Hulse-Taylor binary pulsar system,
leading to the 1993 Nobel Prize in physics awarded to Russell Hulse and Joseph Taylor
for their seminal confirmation of Einstein’s theory through the observation
of the system’s orbital decay due to gravitational wave emission.

However, despite its fundamental significance and successful application to explain
the Hulse-Taylor binary pulsar, the quadrupole formula has been accompanied
by a lingering controversy surrounding its derivation. The theoretical foundation
of the formula was initially met with skepticism, as some researchers questioned the rigor
of its derivation and the validity of the underlying assumptions, cf.
[
11
,
5
]
.
This controversy led to extensive debates within the gravitational physics community,
prompting experts to seek a more comprehensive and rigorous derivation that
would not only resolve the existing concerns but also reaffirm the formula’s relevance
in the era of high precision gravitational wave astronomy.

Our goal in the present work is to rigorously derive the quadrupole formula in the context
of the Einstein-Vlasov system for a compactly supported distribution of collisionless matter.
We start from a set of physically and mathematically motivated assumptions,
and we prove the validity of the approximation by providing an estimate
for its corresponding error term. An important aspect about the assumptions we make
is how these are related to established properties of global solutions of the Einstein-Vlasov system.
More precisely, global existence of solutions for initial data close to Minkowski space
has recently been shown in two independent works
[
9
,
6
]
. The set-up in
[
9
]
,
which relies on harmonic coordinates, is the framework that we are going to use in this paper.
Hence, it is natural to ask if the result in
[
9
]
gives sufficient information
on the solutions to validate the assumptions that we make.

Previous results on the rigorous derivation of the quadrupole formula exist in the literature,
cf.
[
3
,
11
]
. These works are based on a post-Newtonian analysis.
The assumptions on the solutions in these results cannot be compared to mathematically established
global properties of solutions of any Einstein-matter system, e.g. to decay properties
specified in well-defined norms. In fact, no specific matter model is considered there.
A standard application of the quadrupole formula is to binary systems such as the Hulse-Taylor
binary pulsar. Such systems are naturally described by a phenomenological matter model,
in which case global existence results are rare. Vlasov matter constitutes a unique
case of a phenomenological matter model for which global existence has been shown
for small initial data in the case of asymptotically flat spacetimes
[
9
,
6
]
.
(Clearly, for a binary system the assumption about small initial data is not justified
from a physics point of view, but since we aim for rigorous mathematical results
we have to accept this limitation at this point.) We thus find it motivated
to ask what are the mathematical assumptions required on the solutions
to the Einstein-Vlasov system to rigorously derive the quadrupole formula.
Although the result by Lindblad and Taylor
[
9
]
provides a motivation for our study,
it is not sufficient for our goal. One reason for this is related to the well-known

no-incoming-radiation condition
. The purpose of this condition is to make sure
that the matter source is isolated and does not receive any radiation from infinity.
Without this condition there are terms in the expansion used to prove the quadrupole formula
that are infinite. This implies, strictly speaking, that solutions of the Einstein-Vlasov system
have to be established that satisfy the no-incoming-radiation condition;
this is a highly non-trivial problem. Hence, the conditions we impose motivate
further studies on the global properties of solutions to the Einstein-Vlasov system
that go beyond the results obtained in
[
9
,
6
]
. The no-incoming-radiation condition
will be used in a form suggested by Blanchet
[
3
, equ. (19)]
, and it is
discussed in more detail in Section
1.3.1
.

The main result in this work is an estimate for the quadrupole formula derived
from mathematically well-defined assumptions. The error terms in the estimate
are explicitly given and depend on the boundedness and decay assumptions
made on the solution and the distance to the source. Hence, it is possible
to read off the contribution of each error term and to understand its origin.

The paper is organized as follows. In the next two subsections
the Einstein-Vlasov system and the quadrupole formula are introduced.
In Section
1.3
the assumptions on the solutions that we make are specified;
the absence of incoming radiation is discussed in Section
1.3.1

and the decay and boundedness conditions are stated in Section
1.3.2
.
The relation between the assumptions and the global properties of the solutions
that are obtained in
[
9
]
are also discussed. In Section
1.4

we formulate our main result and discuss its meaning. In Section
2
,
and its subsections, we carry out the expansion of the quantities that are needed
to derive our main result. The estimates of the terms in the expansion are carried
out in Section
3
. Some technicalities are brought up in the appendix,
in particular the no-incoming-radiation condition is discussed in Appendix
C
.

1.1
The Einstein-Vlasov system

The Einstein-Vlasov system (with factors of the speed of light
c
and of Newton’s constant

G
included where they belong) is given by Einstein’s equation,

G_{\alpha\beta}=R_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,R\,g_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}\,T_{\alpha\beta},

(1.1)

coupled to the Vlasov equation for a non-negative particle-density function
f
,

p^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}f-\Gamma_{\beta\gamma}^{\alpha}\,p^{\beta}p^{\gamma}\,\partial_{p^{\alpha}}f=0

(1.2)

and to the equation defining the energy-momentum tensor
T_{\alpha\beta}
,

T_{\alpha\beta}=c^{-1}\,|g|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\,f\,\frac{dp^{0}\,dp^{1}\,dp^{2}\,dp^{3}}{m}.

(1.3)

Here
(x^{\alpha},p^{\beta})
are coordinates on the tangent bundle
T{\cal M}

of a four-dimensional spacetime manifold
({\cal M},(g_{\alpha\beta}))
, where
(g_{\alpha\beta})

is a Lorentzian metric of signature
(-,+,+,+)
expressed as

-c^{2}\,d\tau^{2}=ds^{2}=g_{\alpha\beta}\,dx^{\alpha}\,dx^{\beta},

with
\tau
denoting proper time and
t=x^{0}/c
coordinate time. The scalar
g<0

in (
1.3
) denotes the metric determinant, and indices are raised
and lowered using the metric; thus for instance
p_{\alpha}=g_{\alpha\beta}p^{\beta}
.
The scalar
f
in (
1.2
) depends on
(x^{\alpha},p^{\beta})
, i.e.,
f=f(x^{\alpha},p^{\beta})
,
and the Christoffel symbols are given by

\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\delta}(\partial_{x^{\beta}}g_{\gamma\delta}+\partial_{x^{\gamma}}g_{\beta\delta}-\partial_{x^{\delta}}g_{\beta\gamma}).

They are used not only in (
1.2
), but also in the definition

\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\partial_{x^{\beta}}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\gamma}-\partial_{x^{\alpha}}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\gamma}+\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\varepsilon}\,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha\gamma}-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\varepsilon}\,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\beta\gamma}

of the Riemann curvature tensor. Then

R_{\alpha\beta}=\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\alpha}{\gamma}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\alpha}{\gamma}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\alpha}{\gamma}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{\alpha}{\gamma}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

is the Ricci tensor, whereas
R=\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

denotes the scalar curvature (i.e., the trace) of the metric. The Vlasov equation (
1.2
)
expresses the fact that
f
is constant along solutions of the geodesic equations

\frac{dx^{\alpha}}{d\tau}=p^{\alpha},\quad\frac{dp^{\alpha}}{d\tau}=-\Gamma_{\beta\gamma}^{\alpha}\,p^{\beta}p^{\gamma}.

(1.4)

The density function
f
is restricted to the mass shell

P{\cal M}=\{(x^{\mu},p^{\nu})\in T{\cal M}:p^{0}>0,\ g_{\alpha\beta}p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}=-m^{2}c^{2}\}

(1.5)

that is a seven-dimensional geodesically invariant submanifold
of the tangent bundle. We assume that all particles have the same rest mass, i.e.
m=1
. The expression (
1.3
) can then be simplified. Indeed, let us change variables from
(p^{0},p^{1},p^{2},p^{3})
to
(m,\tilde{p}^{1},\tilde{p}^{2},\tilde{p}^{3})
by

\displaystyle p^{0}=p^{0}(m,\tilde{p}^{1},\tilde{p}^{2},\tilde{p}^{3}),

\displaystyle p^{a}=\tilde{p}^{a},\;a=1,2,3.

Here
p^{0}
is given implicitly by the mass shell relation

g_{\alpha\beta}p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}=-m^{2}c^{2},

and we remark that this is always possible in our situation since the solution
we consider is close to Minkowski space where
(p^{0})^{2}=m^{2}c^{2}+(p^{1})^{2}+(p^{2})^{2}+(p^{3})^{2}
.
The Jacobian of the map above is simply
|\partial p^{0}/\partial m|
,
and we get by differentiating the mass shell relation with respect to
m
:

-2mc^{2}=2g_{\alpha 0}p^{\alpha}\frac{\partial p^{0}}{\partial m}=2p_{0}\frac{\partial p_{0}}{\partial m},

so that

\Big|\frac{\partial p_{0}}{\partial m}\Big|=\frac{mc^{2}}{-p_{0}},

where we notice that
-p_{0}>0
. Hence, we find that (
1.3
) takes the form

T_{\alpha\beta}=c\,|g|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\,f\,\frac{dm\,dp^{1}\,dp^{2}\,dp^{3}}{-p_{0}}.

(1.6)

To impose that all the Vlasov-matter particles have constant mass 1 means that
we take
f
of the form
f(x^{\alpha},m,p^{b})=\delta(m-1)\tilde{f}(x^{\alpha},p^{b})
, which results in

T_{\alpha\beta}=c\,|g|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\,f\,\frac{dp^{1}\,dp^{2}\,dp^{3}}{-p_{0}},

(1.7)

where we dropped the tilde, so that
f=f(x^{\alpha},p^{b})
.
To rewrite the Vlasov equation
\frac{d}{d\tau}\,f(x^{\alpha}(\tau),p^{b}(\tau))=0

for fixed mass
m=1
in terms of the coordinate time
t
,
note that
c\,\frac{dt}{d\tau}=\frac{dx^{0}}{d\tau}=p^{0}>0
on
P{\cal M}
. Let

\tilde{x}^{\alpha}(t)=x^{\alpha}(\tau(t)),\quad\tilde{p}^{b}(t)=p^{b}(\tau(t)),\quad\tilde{f}(t,\tilde{x}^{a},\tilde{p}^{b})=f(ct,\tilde{x}^{a},\tilde{p}^{b}).

Then
\tilde{x}^{0}(t)=x^{0}(\tau)=ct
yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\tilde{f}(t,\tilde{x}^{a}(t),\tilde{p}^{b}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,f(ct,\tilde{x}^{a}(t),\tilde{p}^{b}(t))=\frac{d}{dt}\,f(\tilde{x}^{\alpha}(t),\tilde{p}^{b}(t))

(1.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c}{p^{0}}\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\,f(\tilde{x}^{\alpha}(t(\tau)),\tilde{p}^{b}(t(\tau)))=\frac{c}{p^{0}}\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\,f(x^{\alpha}(\tau),p^{b}(\tau))=0.

Since

\frac{d\tilde{x}^{a}}{dt}=\frac{dx^{a}}{d\tau}\,\frac{d\tau}{dt}=\frac{c}{p^{0}}\,\tilde{p}^{a},\quad\frac{d\tilde{p}^{b}}{dt}=\frac{dp^{b}}{d\tau}\,\frac{d\tau}{dt}=-\frac{c}{p^{0}}\,\Gamma_{\beta\gamma}^{b}\,\tilde{p}^{\beta}\tilde{p}^{\gamma},

(1.9)

relation (
1.8
) finally leads to

c^{-1}\partial_{t}f+\frac{p^{a}}{p^{0}}\,\partial_{x^{a}}f-\frac{1}{p^{0}}\,\Gamma_{\beta\gamma}^{a}\,p^{\beta}p^{\gamma}\,\partial_{p^{a}}f=0,

(1.10)

where all tildes have been dropped.

Throughout the paper, what we refer to by the name
Einstein-Vlasov system

is the set of equations (
1.1
), (
1.7
) and (
1.10
)
for the unknowns
g_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{a})
and
f=f(t,x^{a},p^{b})

defined globally as functions of
(t,x^{a})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
(t,x^{a},p^{b})\in\mathbb{R}^{7}
.
It should be noted, however, that in these variables (and re-introducing
the tildes for the moment) relation (
1.7
) is in fact to be read as

\tilde{T}_{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{a})=c\,|g|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\,\tilde{f}(t,x^{a})\,\frac{dp^{1}\,dp^{2}\,dp^{3}}{-p_{0}},

(1.11)

i.e.,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha\beta}(t,x^{a})=T_{\alpha\beta}(ct,x^{a})
. This identification
is also used for other functions throughout the paper.

In order to keep a correct track of relevant physical constants, we let
\lambda>0

be a fixed constant with a dimension of length. Using
\lambda
and the constants
G
and
c
,
we can produce any other dimension relevant to the problem, for example fundamental scales of time,
mass and momentum given respectively by
c^{-1}\lambda
,
G^{-1}\lambda c^{2}
and
G^{-1}\lambda c^{3}
.
In particular, the particles’ constant mass equal to 1 in the above paragraph should be understood
as having the value
G^{-1}\lambda c^{2}
.

1.2
The quadrupole formula

If one considers electromagnetic fields
E,B
(functions of a spacetime argument
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
)
satisfying the Maxwell equations in Minkowski space, then there is a scalar field
e

denoting the electromagnetic-energy density and, given a fixed event
(t,x)
,
it makes sense to define the energy content at time
t
in the ball of radius
r=|x|

and center at the origin by

{\cal E}(t,x)=\int_{B_{r}(0)}e(t,\bar{x})\,d\bar{x}.

(1.12)

Using the divergence theorem, the rate of change of this energy is found to equal the surface integral

\frac{d}{dt}\,{\cal E}(t,x)=\frac{c}{4\pi}\int_{\partial B_{r}(0)}\frac{\bar{x}}{r}\cdot(B\wedge E)(t,\bar{x})\,dS(\bar{x}),

(1.13)

which measures the flow of energy over the boundary to infinity. Reference
[
2
, p. 268/269]

studies the Vlasov-Maxwell and Vlasov-Nordström systems and derives a dipole radiation formula
for the Vlasov-Maxwell case using (
1.13
).

However, in the context of General Relativity, in order to measure the amount
of gravitational energy radiated from a faraway matter source with associated
energy-momentum tensor
T^{\alpha\beta}
, a modification of this approach is needed,
since the gravitational energy is not localizable; see e.g.
[
14
, Section 14]

for further discussion.
For this purpose, we will need the Landau-Lifschitz energy-momentum pseudo tensor
t^{\alpha\beta}
.
Its definition is rather involved and given in (
A.1
),
with Appendix
A
containing the necessary background material.
The upshot in using the energy-momentum pseudo tensor is that it leads
to a physically meaningful notion of the total energy contained in
B_{r}(0)
, namely

\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)=\int_{B_{r}(0)}|g(t,\bar{x})|\,(T^{00}(t,\bar{x})+t^{00}(t,\bar{x}))\,d\bar{x}.

The divergence theorem then implies (see (
A.8
))

\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)=-c\,\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int_{\partial B_{r}(0)}|g(t,\bar{x})|\,\frac{\bar{x}^{i}}{r}\,(T^{0i}(t,\bar{x})+t^{0i}(t,\bar{x}))\,dS((t,\bar{x})),

(1.14)

Therefore (
1.14
) should be viewed as the appropriate replacement for (
1.13
)
when it comes to measuring gravitational radiation.

The
quadrupole-formula approximation
, loosely speaking, is the statement that,
for
x
far enough away from the gravitational source and
t
close enough to
c^{-1}|x|
,
the following approximation holds up to corresponding orders in
h
,
T
and
1/r
terms:

\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)\approx-\frac{G}{5c^{5}}\bigg[\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}(\dddot{D}^{\,jk}(u))^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mathchoice{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(u))^{2}\bigg],

(1.15)

where
u=t-c^{-1}|x|
is the
retarded time
corresponding to the event
(t,x)
, and

D^{\alpha\beta}(u)=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{00}(u,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\,\bar{x}^{\beta}\,d\bar{x}

(1.16)

is the
quadrupole moment
of the gravitational source. The dots denote time derivatives;
thus (note that
\partial_{x^{0}}=c^{-1}\partial_{t}
)

\dddot{D}^{\alpha\beta}(u)=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\partial^{3}_{x^{0}}T_{00}(u,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\,\bar{x}^{\beta}\,d\bar{x}.

Also note that the right-hand side of (
1.15
) is negative, so there
is indeed a loss of gravitational energy.

We assume throughout the paper that global solutions
((g_{\alpha\beta}),f)

to the Einstein-Vlasov system exist satisfying certain technical assumptions,
and our main result, Theorem
1.2
, is an estimate for the error
in the approximation (
1.15
) expressed in terms of powers of
|x|

and of quantities that measure the smallness of
|T^{\alpha\beta}|
and
|h_{\alpha\beta}|

(for the definition of the latter see (
1.17
) just ahead).

1.3
A priori
assumptions on the solutions

In this subsection we explain what the assumptions on our solutions are going to be.

1.3.1
Absence of incoming radiation: Blanchet’s condition

We decompose our metric into the sum of the Minkowski metric and a small perturbation term.
Letting
(\eta_{\alpha\beta})={\rm diag}\,(-1,1,1,1)
and
(\eta^{\alpha\beta})=(\eta_{\alpha\beta})
, the Minkowski metric is given by

-c^{2}d\tau^{2}=ds^{2}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}\,dx^{\alpha}\,dx^{\beta}=-c^{2}\,dt^{2}+(dx^{1})^{2}+(dx^{2})^{2}+(dx^{3})^{2},

where
x^{0}=ct
as before, and we define the following coefficients measuring
the deviation of
g
from
\eta
:

h_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha\beta}-\eta_{\alpha\beta}\quad\mbox{as well as}\quad H^{\alpha\beta}=g^{\alpha\beta}-\eta^{\alpha\beta}.

(1.17)

We also let

h^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad h=\mathchoice{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{h^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}h_{\alpha\beta},

(1.18)

and furthermore

\bar{h}_{\alpha\beta}=h_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,h\,\eta_{\alpha\beta},

(1.19)

so that

h_{\alpha\beta}=\bar{h}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,\bar{h}\,\eta_{\alpha\beta}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bar{h}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,\bar{h}_{\alpha\beta}=-h.

(1.20)

Finally, we put

\bar{h}^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\bar{h}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}},

(1.21)

and hence, cf. (
1.18
),

h^{\alpha\beta}=\bar{h}^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,\bar{h}\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}=\bar{h}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,h\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}

(1.22)

is verified.

In order for the quantity approximated by the quadrupole formula to truly represent
the rate of change of energy radiated from the source matter, it is necessary
that the spacetime metric satisfy a condition that can be physically interpreted
as meaning the absence of radiation incoming from spatial infinity. For the solutions
considered in this paper, we are going to assume the following condition that is inspired
by (and very close to) the one put forward by Blanchet
[
3
, equ. (19)]
:

No-incoming-radiation (NIR) condition:

We say that a solution
to the Einstein-Vlasov system is
isolated from incoming radiation
,
if there exist parameters
{\cal T}>0
and
{\cal S}>0
and functions

p_{\alpha\beta}=p_{\alpha\beta}(x)
such that for
\alpha,\beta=0,1,2,3

h_{\alpha\beta}(t,x)=p_{\alpha\beta}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad|\partial^{\kappa}p_{\alpha\beta}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{|\kappa|}}\frac{\delta_{h}}{|x|^{1+|\kappa|}}.

(1.23)

for all
t
with
t\leq-{\cal T}
, all
x
with
|x|\geq{\cal S}

and all multi-indices
\kappa
with
|\kappa|\leq 3
. Here
\delta_{h}
denotes
a universal constant depending on the particular solution, but not on
t
,
x
or
\kappa
,
which is used to measure “smallness”.

The relations (
1.23
) will render several of the terms that appear
in our estimates technically tractable. A more thorough discussion of Blanchet’s condition
and how it relates to our NIR condition above can be found in Appendix
C
.

1.3.2
Support and decay properties

Through the recent papers
[
6
]
and
[
9
]
it is known that Minkowski space
is asymptotically stable with respect to small perturbations of the Einstein-Vlasov system,
where the “smallness” has to be specified in technical terms. The solutions that
we are going to consider are modeled after the work
[
9
]
, which shows global existence
of the Einstein-Vlasov system posed as a Cauchy problem with compactly supported
and sufficiently smooth initial data
f_{0}(x,p)=f(0,x,p)
, with the solutions additionally
satisfying certain support properties for
f
and
T^{\alpha\beta}
as well
as decay properties for
T^{\alpha\beta}
and
h_{\alpha\beta}
. In particular,
as in that work, we will be using the harmonic gauge condition
\Box_{g}x^{\lambda}=0
;
see Appendix
B
for more details on this gauge.

The following conditions express the fact that the Vlasov matter propagates
at a subluminal speed that remains bounded. See Lemma
5.1
and Corollary
5.2

in Appendix
E
to see how these conditions can be derived
from a natural assumption on the support of the initial data
f_{0}
for the Einstein-Vlasov system.

Compact-support conditions:

We say that a solution
to the Einstein-Vlasov system satisfies the
compact-support conditions

when there exist positive parameters
\beta_{0}
,
K_{0}
,
K_{0}^{\prime}

and
K_{\ast}
, with
\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
, such that:

(a)

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathrm{supp}(f(t,\cdot,\cdot))\subset\Big\{(x,p):|x|\leq\lambda K_{0}+\beta_{0}c|t|,\ |p|\leq c(K^{\prime}_{0}+1)\Big\}.

(1.24)

(b)

For all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
|t-c^{-1}|x||\leq\lambda c^{-1}

and all
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|\bar{x}|\geq\lambda K_{\ast}
,

T_{\alpha\beta}(t-c^{-1}|x-\bar{x}|,\ \bar{x})=0\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\alpha\beta}(t,\bar{x})=0,

(1.25)

the same also being true for any derivatives of
T_{\alpha\beta}
,
as well as for
T^{\alpha\beta}
and its derivatives.

We remark that Corollary
5.2
will show that (b) above follows from (a) if one defines

K_{\ast}=\frac{K_{0}+\beta_{0}}{1-\beta_{0}},

(1.26)

whereas (a), for solutions evolving from initial data satisfying appropriate conditions,
holds for a given
K_{0}
and
K_{0}^{\prime}
if
\beta_{0}
is defined by the relation

1-\beta_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{4(8K^{\prime}_{0}+1)}.

(1.27)

Next, to describe the final set of conditions, once again following
[
9
]
, we need a further splitting
of the weak-field coefficients
h_{\alpha\beta}
into a Schwarzschild-like piece and a remainder piece. Let

\chi:[0,\infty)\longrightarrow[0,1]\quad\text{such that }\chi(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{ for }\xi\leq 1/2,\\
1&\text{ for }\xi\geq 3/4\\
\end{array}\right.

(1.28)

be a smooth cutoff function. Given a solution to the Einstein-Vlasov system with corresponding
ADM mass
M
as well as
h_{\alpha\beta}
defined in (
1.17
), let

h^{0}_{\alpha\beta}(x)=\chi\left(\frac{|x|}{\lambda+|t|}\right)\frac{GM}{c^{2}|x|}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\ ,\quad h^{1}_{\alpha\beta}(x)=h_{\alpha\beta}(x)-h^{0}_{\alpha\beta}(x).

(1.29)

In Appendix
D
, Corollary
4.3
, we outline the relevant estimates from
[
9
]

that motivate the following conditions. For now it suffices to say that they can be derived from
a set of natural assumptions concerning the smallness of the initial data of the Einstein-Vlasov system.

Boundedness and decay conditions:

We say that a solution
to the Einstein-Vlasov system satisfies the
boundedness and decay conditions

if there exist parameters
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\delta_{h}>0

and
\delta_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}
(the latter for each
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3

and multi-index
\kappa
with
|\kappa|\leq 4
) such that, for all
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3
,
all
(x^{0},x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
(representing harmonic-gauge coordinates) and all multi-indices
\kappa

with
|\kappa|\leq 4
,

|\partial^{\kappa}T_{\mu\nu}(x^{0},x)|\leq\frac{c^{4}}{G\lambda^{2+|\kappa|}}\,\delta_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa},

(1.30)

|\partial h_{\mu\nu}(x^{0},x)|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\delta_{h}}{\big(1+\lambda^{-1}|x^{0}|\big)^{1-2\delta}\ \big(1+\lambda^{-1}\big||x|-|x^{0}|\big|\big)},

(1.31)

|\partial^{\kappa}(h^{1})_{\mu\nu}(x^{0},x)|\leq\dfrac{1}{\lambda^{|\kappa|}}\cdot\dfrac{\delta_{h}(1+\lambda^{-1}|x^{0}|)^{\delta_{h}}}{\big(1+\lambda^{-1}(|x|+|x^{0}|)\big)\ \big(1+\lambda^{-1}(|x|-|x^{0}|)_{+}\big)^{\gamma}},

(1.32)

where
\partial h_{\mu\nu}
stands for
any of the four first-order derivatives of
h_{\mu\nu}
,
and
(|x|-|x^{0}|)_{+}
means
|x|-|x^{0}|
if
|x|\geq|x^{0}|
and zero otherwise.

For a more finely tuned result, one could also assume modified bounds
on the different
\partial^{\kappa}h_{\mu\nu}
by introducing separate constants

\delta_{h,\mu\nu}^{(\kappa)}
for them, but we refrain from doing so. For our purposes here,
this kind of separation will only be needed for the
\partial^{\kappa}T_{\mu\nu}
terms,
as above. Also note that, for the proof of Theorem
1.2
, we do not assume
any
a priori
relation between the constants
\delta_{h}

and
\delta_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}
(but cf.
[
11
, p. 330]
).

Remark 1.1

Since our intention is to study an isolated collisionless gravitational system
close to Minkowski space, it makes sense to consider solutions
to the Einstein-Vlasov system satisfying the conditions (
1.23
), (
1.24
),
(
1.25
) and (
1.30
)–(
1.32
).
Let us clearly state that we do not know at present if such solutions do actually exist,
and in particular that we do not show this in the present paper.

In a similar vein, it is generally accepted
that a “no incoming radiation condition” has to be imposed
in order to not obtain unwanted and unphysical contributions.
For this, we take a formulation of such a condition (set out by Blanchet, as introduced above)
that still is manageable analytically. It has to be clarified in future work,
if the NIR condition (
1.23
) could be replaced by an asymptotic condition as
t\to-\infty
.

Put together, we currently do not know to which solutions our main result applies,
but we nevertheless consider this work to be a valid initial step,
since the quadrupole formula is derived from clear-cut assumptions.

1.4
Statement of the main result

The main result of this paper is the next theorem.

Theorem 1.2

Let positive constants
C_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\mathcal{T}\geq 2c^{-1}\lambda
,

\mathcal{S}\geq\max\{2\mathcal{T}c,6\lambda\}
,
K_{0}
,
K_{0}^{\prime}
,
\gamma\in[\frac{3}{4},1)

and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
be given, and define
\beta_{0}
as in (
1.27
)
and
K_{\ast}
as in (
1.26
). Furthermore, suppose that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,
there exist constants

\varepsilon_{h}\in(0,\varepsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}\in(0,\varepsilon)

(for each
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3
and each multi-index
\kappa
such that
|\kappa|\leq 4
)
and a set of initial data
(g_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)},\partial_{t}g_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)},f_{0}^{(\varepsilon)})\big|_{t=0}
for the Einstein-Vlasov system satisfying

\mathrm{supp}(f_{0}^{(\varepsilon)})\subset\{(x,p):|x|\leq\lambda K_{0},\ |p|\leq cK^{\prime}_{0}\}

and leading to a globally defined solution
(g_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)},f^{(\varepsilon)})

in harmonic coordinates with the following properties:

(i)

the NIR condition (
1.23
) for
h_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)}=g_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)}-\eta_{\alpha\beta}
holds with the constant
\delta_{h}=C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}
;

(ii)

the compact-support conditions (
1.24
) and (
1.25
)
hold for
f^{(\varepsilon)}
and the corresponding
T_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)}
;

(iii)

the boundedness and decay conditions (
1.31
)–(
1.32
)
hold for
T_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)}
,
h^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mu\nu}
and
h^{(\varepsilon),1}_{\mu\nu}

(the latter defined as in (
1.29
)) with the constants

\delta_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}=C_{0}\varepsilon_{T,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}
and
\delta_{h}=C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}
;

(iv)

the ADM mass
M^{(\varepsilon)}
corresponding to the metric
g^{(\varepsilon)}
satisfies

\frac{GM^{(\varepsilon)}}{c^{2}\lambda}<\varepsilon_{h}.

(1.33)

Then there are
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and a constant
C_{1}>0

such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
and events
(t,x)
so that

|x|\geq\max\{12\lambda,c{\cal T},2{\cal S},5\lambda K_{\ast}\}\quad\text{and}\quad|u|=|t-c^{-1}|x||\leq\lambda c^{-1},

the following quadrupole approximation for gravitational radiation holds
for the solution
(g_{\alpha\beta}^{(\varepsilon)},f^{(\varepsilon)})
:

\displaystyle\bigg|\partial_{t}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)-\bigg(-\frac{G}{5c^{5}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}\bigg[(\dddot{D}^{\,jk}(u))^{2}-\frac{1}{3}(\mathchoice{\dddot{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(u))^{2}\bigg]\bigg)\bigg|

(1.34)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\,\frac{c^{5}}{G}\,\Bigg[(\lambda^{-1}|x|)^{1-\gamma_{0}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}}\,\varepsilon_{h}^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{-1}|x|}\,\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}\varepsilon_{T}^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\,\Big(\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(3),x^{0}}+\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(4),x^{0}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(4),x^{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(4),x^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt+\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(3),x^{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(3),x^{0}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(2),x^{0}}+\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}\varepsilon_{T}^{(j)}\Big)\varepsilon_{h}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\times\Bigg[(\lambda^{-1}|x|)^{1-\gamma_{0}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}}\,\varepsilon_{h}^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{-1}|x|}\,\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}\varepsilon_{T}^{(j)}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\,\Big(\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(4),x^{0}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(4),x^{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(4),x^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0001pt+\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(3),x^{0}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(3),x^{0}}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(2),x^{0}}+\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}\varepsilon_{T}^{(j)}\Big)\varepsilon_{h}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\,C_{1}\,\frac{c^{5}}{G}\,\frac{1}{(\lambda^{-1}|x|)^{1-3\varepsilon_{h}}}\,\varepsilon_{h}^{3},

where we have used the symbols

\varepsilon_{T}^{(j)}=\max\{\varepsilon_{T,\,\mu\nu}^{\kappa}:|\kappa|=j,\ \mu,\nu=0,1,2,3\}

and

\varepsilon_{T,\,\mu\nu}^{(j),x^{0}}=\varepsilon_{T,\,\mu\nu}^{(0\ldots 0)},

i.e., the multi-index
\kappa
corresponding to the latter has
j
entries all equal to 0.

A word on the proof of this result: Through a sequence of interrelated remainder terms
(of which we will keep precise track), we will carve out the quadrupole expression
from
\partial_{t}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)
, culminating at the relation (
2.93
), namely

\partial_{t}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}(t,x)=-\frac{G}{5c^{5}}\bigg[\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}(\dddot{D}^{\,jk}(u))^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mathchoice{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\dddot{D}^{{{jj}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.42712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.59668pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(u))^{2}\bigg]+({\rm rem}_{50})(t,x).

(1.35)

The term at the end is a remainder term, the remainder number 50 in our estimates.
As soon as it is bounded in (
3.74
), the proof of the theorem will be complete.

Remark 1.3

(a) We now explain why the estimate (
1.34
) is meaningful with respect
to the relative sizes of the objects
being compared. The left-hand side is the difference between two expressions
whose orders of magnitude are as follows, in self-explanatory symbolic notation:

\partial_{t}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}\sim t^{0i}\sim(\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\bar{h}^{0i})(\partial_{x^{\delta}}\bar{h}^{\delta\gamma})\sim(\partial_{x^{\gamma}}h_{0i})(\partial_{x^{\delta}}h_{\delta\gamma})\sim\delta_{h}^{2}

(1.36)

and

(\dddot{D}^{\,jk})^{2}\sim(\partial_{x^{0}}^{3}T_{00})^{2}\sim(\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(3),x^{0}})^{2}.

(1.37)

Let us check that the right-hand side of (
1.34
) has a smaller order of magnitude
in comparison to (
1.36
) and (
1.37
). The last term in there
is of order
\sim\frac{1}{|x|^{1-3C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}}}\,\delta_{h}^{3}
and thus can be expected
to be “small” as compared to the terms on the left-hand side. As for the

[\ldots]\times[\ldots]
term, there is the factor
|x|^{1-\gamma_{0}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}}\varepsilon_{h}^{2}

that leads to at least
{\cal O}(\varepsilon^{3})
upon multiplication with another factor.
However,
1-\gamma_{0}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}>0
for
\varepsilon_{h}
small,
so there is a positive power of
|x|
to deal with when discussing
the range of validity of the quadrupole approximation; also recall that we are allowed
to take
\gamma_{0}<1
as close to
1
as we wish initially. It should be stressed that the positive power
is
not
an artifact of the proof, but actually the best bound that
one can get under the decay assumptions that we impose on
h
.
From a mathematics-of-wave-equations perspective,
the estimate perhaps could be improved to something like
\log(3+|x|)
,
but this would not make a conceptual difference, since the whole point
is that the available estimates on
h
degenerate close to the light cone; see (
1.31
)
and (
1.32
). Certainly, if there were rigorous results that showed an improved decay for
h
,
the factor
|x|^{1-\gamma_{0}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{h}}
would disappear. For the other orders,
in a physically realistic slow-motion situation, one expects to have

|T_{00}|\gg|T_{0j}|\gg|T_{ij}|,

see
[
14
, p. 222]
or
[
11
, p. 330]
. In terms of a “typical velocity”
\bar{v}

in the system and under the slow-motion assumption
(\frac{\bar{v}}{c})\ll 1
, it is the case that

\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{2}|T_{00}|\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)|T_{0j}|\sim|T_{ij}|,

which can be seen from (
1.7
) and the fact that
\sim-p_{0}f\sim\sqrt{1+|p|^{2}}f

appears integrated in the expression for
T_{00}
,
\sim p_{j}f\sim\sqrt{1+|p|^{2}}v_{j}f

is integrated in the expression for
T_{0j}

and
\sim\frac{p_{i}p_{j}}{\sqrt{1+|p|^{2}}}f\sim\sqrt{1+|p|^{2}}v_{i}v_{j}f
is integrated
in the expression for
T_{ij}
. Furthermore,

\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\sim\frac{1}{\lambda},\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{0}}\sim\frac{(\bar{v}/c)}{\lambda},

for taking derivatives
[
15
, p. 303]
.
Therefore the following orders are obtained:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(3),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{3}|T_{00}|,\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(4),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{4}|T_{00}|,\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(4),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{6}|T_{00}|,\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(4),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{5}|T_{00}|,

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(3),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{5}|T_{00}|,\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(3),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{4}|T_{00}|,\quad\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(2),x^{0}}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{4}|T_{00}|.

Hence, if we look at the different
{\cal O}(\varepsilon^{2})
-products
that appear on the right-hand side of (
1.34
), the worst order results from

\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(3),x^{0}}(\varepsilon_{T,\,00}^{(4),x^{0}}+\varepsilon_{T,\,0j}^{(3),x^{0}}+\varepsilon_{T,\,ij}^{(2),x^{0}})\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{7}|T_{00}|^{2},

which is one order better than

(\dddot{D}^{\,jk})^{2}\sim\Big(\frac{\bar{v}}{c}\Big)^{6}|T_{00}|^{2},

and thus indeed “small”.

(b) We will not carry out an explicit post-Newtonian expansion “
c\to\infty
”
in this paper, but it should be remarked that the Newtonian limit
of the Einstein-Vlasov system is the Vlasov-Poisson system;
see
[
13
,
12
]
, and also
[
14
, p. 176-178]

and
[
15
, p. 219/220]
. If
\rho_{{\rm VP}}(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{{\rm VP}}(t,x,v)\,dv

and
U_{{\rm VP}}
denote the density and the Newtonian potential
for this Vlasov-Poisson system, respectively, then one readily sees

U_{{\rm VP}}\sim-\frac{c^{2}}{2}h_{00}
and
T_{00}\sim c^{2}\rho_{{\rm VP}}
,
so that
D^{\alpha\beta}\sim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{{\rm VP}}\,x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}\,dx
.

2
Expansion of the energy-rate formula

In this section we carry out the expansion (up to the needed orders with respect
to powers of the terms
1/|x|
and
T^{\alpha\beta}
) of all the basic quantities
that enter the formula (
1.14
) for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{p}^{0}
.
Most physics-oriented references perform this expansion by simply discarding terms
that are deemed small due to being of a high order in those terms, but it should be noted
that our goal here is to keep precise track of these errors, which will then be treated
in Section
3
. We call these terms
remainder terms

and denote them by the symbol
({\rm rem}_{i})
, and the point is to use only those bounds
that can be justified mathematically.

Let a solution to the Einstein-Vlasov system satisfy the NIR condition (
1.23
),
compact-support conditions (
1.24
), (
1.25
)
and decay conditions (
1.30
)–(
1.32
), and let
(t,x)
be a fixed spacetime
event such that
|u|=|t-c^{-1}|x||\leq\lambda c^{-1}
and
r=|x|\geq\lambda K_{\ast}
.

2.1
Einstein tensor

According to (
B.33
) in Lemma
2.2
, the Ricci tensor
in the harmonic gauge is given by

R_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box^{\prime}_{g}\,g_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{P}(g;\partial_{x^{\alpha}}g,\partial_{x^{\beta}}g)+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(g;\partial g,\partial g),

where
\Box^{\prime}_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}}
;
the expressions
\tilde{P}
and
\tilde{Q}

are defined in (
B.31
) and (
B.32
).
Since
\partial_{x^{\gamma}}g_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{x^{\gamma}}h_{\alpha\beta}
,
we first observe that

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box^{\prime}_{g}\,h_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{P}(g;\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(g;\partial h,\partial h)

(2.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box^{\prime}_{g}\,h_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,P(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial h,\partial h)+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta},

where

P(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)=\tilde{P}(\eta;\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h),\quad Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial h,\partial h)=\tilde{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(\eta;\partial h,\partial h),

as well as

{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{P}(g;\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)-\frac{1}{2}\,P(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(g;\partial h,\partial h)-\frac{1}{2}\,Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial h,\partial h),

(2.2)

which is a remainder term that is (formally) cubic in
h
. The relation (
2.1
)
corresponds to
[
8
, Lemma 3.2]
. Explicitly, (
B.31
) and (
B.32
) yield

\displaystyle P(\partial_{x^{\mu}}h,\partial_{x^{\nu}}h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}},

\displaystyle Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h,\partial h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\beta\mu}\,\partial_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}}h_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}

\displaystyle-\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\beta\mu}\,\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\beta\mu}\,\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu})

\displaystyle+\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,(\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\beta\nu})

\displaystyle+\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,(\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\beta\mu})

\displaystyle+\,\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,(\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{x^{\mu}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\beta\nu})

\displaystyle+\,\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\eta^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,(\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{x^{\nu}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h_{\beta\mu}).

If one is interested only in an expansion up to quadratic errors in
h
,
then using (
1.17
) in (
2.1
) gives

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,(\eta^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}})\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}x^{\beta^{\prime}}}\,h_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,P(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial h,\partial h)+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}

(2.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box\,h_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta},

defining
\Box=\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\alpha}x^{\beta}}
, and furthermore

{({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\,P(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial h,\partial h),\quad{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\,H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}x^{\beta^{\prime}}}\,h_{\alpha\beta}.

(2.4)

The term
{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta}
is also expected to be small,
since
H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=(H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+h^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}})-h^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
,
where the term in parentheses turns out to be quadratic in
h
. For the scalar curvature, this implies

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}

(2.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\eta^{\alpha\beta}+H^{\alpha\beta})\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box\,h_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,\Box\,h_{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box\,h+({\rm rem}_{3})

for

\displaystyle({\rm rem})_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,({({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta})

\displaystyle+\,H^{\alpha\beta}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box\,h_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta}\Big).

Thus it follows from (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) that the Einstein tensor can be written as

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,R\,g_{\alpha\beta}

(2.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\Box\,\Big(h_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,h\,\eta_{\alpha\beta}\Big)+{({\rm rem}_{4})}_{\alpha\beta},

where

\displaystyle{({\rm rem}_{4})}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{({\rm rem}_{0})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{1})}_{\alpha\beta}+{({\rm rem}_{2})}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\,({\rm rem}_{3})\,g_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{4}\,(\Box\,h)\,h_{\alpha\beta}

(2.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{P}(g;\partial_{x^{\alpha}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta}}h)+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{Q}_{\alpha\beta}(g;\partial h,\partial h)-\frac{1}{2}\,H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}x^{\beta^{\prime}}}\,h_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{4}\,(\eta^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}})\bigg(\tilde{P}(g;\partial_{x^{\alpha^{\prime}}}h,\partial_{x^{\beta^{\prime}}}h)+\tilde{Q}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(g;\partial h,\partial h)-H^{\gamma\delta}\,\partial^{2}_{x^{\gamma}x^{\delta}}\,h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\bigg)\,g_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\,\frac{1}{4}\,\Big(H^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\Box\,h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\Big)\,g_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{4}\,(\Box\,h)\,h_{\alpha\beta}

is quadratic in
h
.

2.2
Metric determinant

Let
\hat{g}=(g_{\alpha\beta})
,
\hat{m}=(\eta_{\alpha\beta})

and
\hat{h}=(h_{\alpha\beta})
be viewed as matrices of tensor components. Then

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det\hat{g}=\det(\hat{m}+\hat{h})=\det(\hat{m})\det(I+\hat{m}^{-1}\hat{h})=-\det(I+\hat{m}^{-1}\hat{h})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\det\bigg(I+\|(h_{\alpha\beta})\|\,\hat{m}^{-1}\frac{\hat{h}}{\|(h_{\alpha\beta})\|}\bigg)=-1-\|(h_{\alpha\beta})\|\,{\rm tr}\,\Big(\hat{m}^{-1}\frac{\hat{h}}{\|(h_{\alpha\beta})\|}\Big)+({\rm rem}_{5})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1-\,{\rm tr}\,(\hat{m}^{-1}\hat{h})+({\rm rem}_{5})=-1-h-({\rm rem}_{5})

for

({\rm rem}_{5})=\det(I+\hat{m}^{-1}\hat{h})-1-{\rm tr}\,(\hat{m}^{-1}\hat{h}),

(2.8)

cf. also (
1.18
). It follows that

|g|=-g=1+h+({\rm rem}_{5})

and hence

|g|^{1/2}=\sqrt{1+h+({\rm rem}_{5})}=1+\frac{1}{2}\,h+({\rm rem}_{6}),\quad({\rm rem}_{6})=\sqrt{1+h+({\rm rem}_{5})}-1-\frac{1}{2}\,h.

(2.9)

2.3
Energy-momentum pseudo tensor

Our restrictions
|t-c^{-1}r|<\lambda c^{-1}
and
r\geq\lambda K_{\ast}
(where
r=|x|
)
imply through the compact-support condition (
1.25
)
that the
T^{0i}=T^{0i}(t,\bar{x})
term in the surface-integral formula (
1.14
)
for
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{p}^{0}
vanishes. Thus

\partial_{t}\,\mathfrak{p}^{0}=-c\,\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int_{\partial B_{r}(0)}|g|\,\frac{x^{i}}{r}\,t^{0i}\,dS(x).

(2.10)

We remind the reader that the Landau-Lifschitz energy-momentum pseudo tensor

t^{\alpha\beta}
is defined in (
A.1
). Its components are given in terms
of derivatives of the metric components as well as of the quantity

\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\beta}=|g|^{1/2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}
. Using (
A.2
),
(
2.9
), (
1.17
) and (
1.22
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|g|^{1/2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}\,h+({\rm rem}_{6})\Big)(\eta^{\alpha\beta}+H^{\alpha\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}\,h+({\rm rem}_{6})\Big)\Big(\eta^{\alpha\beta}-h^{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{7})^{\alpha\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{\alpha\beta}-h^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,h\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{8})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{\alpha\beta}-\bar{h}^{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{8})^{\alpha\beta}

for

\displaystyle({\rm rem}_{7})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=h^{\alpha\beta}+H^{\alpha\beta},

(2.11)

\displaystyle({\rm rem}_{8})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,h\,h^{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{7})^{\alpha\beta}\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}\,h\Big)+({\rm rem}_{6})\Big(\eta^{\alpha\beta}-h^{\alpha\beta}+({\rm rem}_{7})^{\alpha\beta}\Big).

(2.12)

As a consequence,

\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\beta}=-\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\bar{h}^{\alpha\beta}+\mathchoice{({\rm (2.13)

defining

rem}_{8})^{\alpha\beta}.

(2.14)

Therefore (
A.1
) in conjunction with (
2.13
) and (
1.17
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{16\pi G}{c^{4}}\,|g|\,t^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{x^{\delta}}\mathfrak{g}^{\delta\gamma}-\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\gamma}\,\partial_{x^{\delta}}\mathfrak{g}^{\beta\delta}+\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\,g_{\delta\varepsilon}\,\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\delta\gamma}\,\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-g_{\varepsilon\gamma}\,\partial_{x^{\delta}}\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon\sigma}\,(g^{\alpha\delta}\,\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\beta\gamma}+g^{\beta\delta}\,\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\gamma})+g_{\delta\varepsilon}\,g^{\gamma\sigma}\,\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\beta\delta}\,\partial_{x^{\sigma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\alpha\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}\,(2g^{\alpha\delta}\,g^{\beta\gamma}-g^{\alpha\beta}\,g^{\delta\gamma})(2g_{\varepsilon\sigma}\,g_{\kappa\nu}-g_{\sigma\kappa}\,g_{\varepsilon\nu})\,\partial_{x^{\delta}}\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon\nu}\,\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\mathfrak{g}^{\sigma\kappa}

\displaystyle=

For mixed time/space indices this can be simplified as

\displaystyle\frac{16\pi G}{c^{4}}\,|g|\,t^{0i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{x^{i}}\bar{h}^{00}\,\partial_{x^{0}}\bar{h}^{00}+\partial_{x^{j}}\bar{h}^{0i}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{kj}-\partial_{x^{0}}\bar{h}^{00}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{ik}-\partial_{x^{j}}\bar{h}^{0j}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{ik}+\partial_{x^{i}}\bar{h}^{0j}\,\partial_{x^{j}}\bar{h}^{00}

(2.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{0}}\bar{h}^{jk}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{ij}-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{i}}\bar{h}^{jk}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{0j}-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{j}}\bar{h}^{i0}\,\partial_{x^{j}}\bar{h}^{00}+\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{ij}\,\partial_{x^{k}}\bar{h}^{0j}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{0}}\bar{h}^{jk}\,\partial_{x^{i}}\bar{h}^{jk}+\frac{1}{4}\,\partial_{x^{0}}\bar{h}\,\partial_{x^{i}}\bar{h}+({\rm rem}_{10})^{0i}.

2.4
Moments

We define, following
[
14
, p. 223]
, the auxiliary functions

\displaystyle m(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{00}(t,\bar{x})\,d\bar{x},\quad d^{\alpha}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{00}(t,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\,d\bar{x},\quad d^{\alpha\beta}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{00}(t,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\bar{x}^{\beta}\,d\bar{x},\qquad

(2.17)

\displaystyle-p_{\nu}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{0\nu}(t,\bar{x})\,d\bar{x},\quad\mathchoice{b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{b^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{0\nu}(t,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\,d\bar{x},\quad a_{\alpha\beta}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T_{\alpha\beta}(t,\bar{x})\,d\bar{x},\qquad

(2.18)

as well as

\tilde{d}^{\,\alpha\beta}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T^{00}(t,\bar{x})\,\bar{x}^{\alpha}\bar{x}^{\beta}\,d\bar{x},\quad\tilde{a}^{\,\alpha\beta}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}T^{\alpha\beta}(t,\bar{x})\,d\bar{x}.

imply
\nabla_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
, where
\nabla
denotes the covariant derivative. From this identity follows
